[00:57:24] road was closed through vehicles. There are a few steps for the road to be fully discontinued. The board needs to have a written petition to VDOT supporting discontinuance. [01:17:12] Coming up. All right. Good evening, board. Like I said, name is Noah Ellington. I've had a great privilege serving Almar County this summer. Tonight, I am here to present the Biophilic Cities Network, a global organization of cities founded by UVA's own Tim Beatley, who it would be sitting in the audience if we were doing this at 6 o'clock, but I told him really quickly to hop online if he can.

[01:17:41] So if not, he'll be able to live stream it. but we had a great conversation about this network. For a purpose of information, the board did receive a letter on May 20th of this year from the chair of the National World Heritage Committee recommending Albemarle County to apply for membership.

[01:17:59] So the Biophilic Cities Network is a group of more than 30 localities across the globe who promote human-nature connections. It is a vision-driven organization focused on inspiration and idea sharing and not on limiting local action. The Biophilic Cities Network recognizes that humans have co-evolved with the natural world and that we have an innate need for contact with nature and other forms of life.

[01:18:26] The mission of a biophilic city is to create equitable opportunities for people to learn about and connect with nature. Such a city places nature at the core of its design and planning functions, which encourages placemaking and sustainable urban design.

[01:18:42] Additionally, it protects, grows, and celebrates local nature and seeks to inform its citizens on the importance of biodiversity. Decades of research confirm that contact with nature reduces stress, improves mental health, and sharpens cognitive performance.

[01:18:59] Even short, daily exposure to green space can measurably enhance well-being, especially for children and those in under-resourced communities. Cities that invest in biophilic design see real returns. Green spaces can help manage heat and flooding, improve air quality, support biodiversity, and lower public health costs.

[01:19:22] They also make communities more attractive by boosting property values and drawing residents and businesses in seeking a high quality of life. There are many benefits to being a part of the network. For one, members will have access to an online repository full of articles, reports, and policy guidance from peer cities.

[01:19:45] There are a host of engagement opportunities, ranging from monthly webinars, partner hosted by partners to annual conferences. Additionally, membership in the network provides the board and staff opportunities to visit other cities to see their biophilic initiatives or even host a delegation here in Albemarle County. It should not be understated that this opportunity could strengthen the county's connection with the University of Virginia faculty and staff, which does have the potential for downstream benefits. This opportunity coincides

[01:20:21] with Albemarle County's strategic plan, particularly objectives relating to quality of life. More specifically, membership in the network can help fulfill the last sub-goal about access to parks and recreational opportunities. There are also indirect implications for economic development and education, which are other goals in the strategic plan.

[01:20:44] I thought it would interest the board to see where on a map these cities are located, since I can't begin to list all of them out. Most of them are in the global north, as you can see. The majority of cities are here in the United States, but there are also several in Europe and Asia. Notably, there is a cluster of dots along the eastern seaboard and particularly around Almaro County. So cities in the network are of various sizes. The largest is Singapore with six million inhabitants, and the smallest is Fremantle, which is part of the Perth metropolitan area in

[01:21:21] Australia with 36,000 residents. There are four localities in Virginia that are part of the network, including Arlington, Reston, Norfolk, and Richmond. Their populations are listed on the slide based on five-year estimates of the 2023 ACS data. In April of this year, the Charlottesville City Council initiated their application process by adopting a resolution supporting the goals of the Biophilic Cities Network. I then wanted to show the board a few examples of nearby partner localities and how they have incorporated biophilia. I had the privilege of speaking with the staff contacts at

[01:21:59] each of these localities and i'm very grateful for their time so first is arlington county they joined in 2020 i spoke with them about what membership and the network means to the organization they acknowledged the benefits of this sort of self-reflection period and told me that it reinforced several existing policies including their minimum tree canopy ordinance which dates back to i believe 2002 in 2022 the county adopted a redevelopment plan for the pentagon city neighborhood that includes biophilic design through a set of guidelines, including an emphasis

[01:22:33] on plantings and open spaces along what they call the green ribbon. The county also holds annual internal workshops and programs, which serves as a mini conference for sharing ideas on how they can become more biophilic. Second is the city of Norfolk. They joined in 2019. The city is currently working on NFK 2050, which is their new comprehensive plan that incorporates biophilic principles in every chapter in the regular planning process. They also adopted a minimum tree canopy zoning

[01:23:07] amendment using language and expertise from Arlington's policy and staff. The last one that I have to share is a little bit closer to my hometown, City of Raleigh, joined in 2022. That same year, the city launched a program to identify areas that lack street trees, and they offered residents free plantings. So over the past three years, staff have planted more than 1,000 street trees through this effort, and the map of that is below, or on the slide, excuse me. The city is also in the process of updating their comprehensive plan with a renewed focus on biophilic principles

[01:23:45] and strategies. So to recap, there are several advantages to joining the Biophilic Cities Network. This initiative can help inform ongoing future and future land use policies, reinforcing many of the goals already outlined in the AC44 Comprehensive Plan draft. It also aligns with other adopted efforts such as the Climate Action Plan and the Biodiversity Action Plan. Participation requires tracking performance metrics, which supports the ongoing work of the SPEAR reporting team.

[01:24:20] Joining also provides a framework for recognizing the value of both our developed and rural areas while offering tools to address urban challenges such as heat islands, mobility impediments, and environmental inequality. Additionally, as Ann mentioned, joining the network will not require a new position to be created or cause a significant workload increase for staff. Importantly, this is an aspirational, non-binding commitment, and there are no penalties from the network if the board has to make difficult policy decisions in the future. So staff is seeking guidance from the board on

[01:24:58] whether to pursue membership in the Biophilic Cities Network. If the board is supportive, the next step would be adopting a resolution acknowledging the network's goals. From there, staff would begin the application process, which involves submitting a narrative of the current initiatives that the county is undergoing, aspirational goals for the county, and five metrics that the county will track to approach those goals. While there is no membership fee, members are expected to submit an annual report on those selected metrics. And so, again, thank you for the opportunity to present

[01:42:26] Bill Fritz, Development Process Manager with the Community Development Department. And you can take it away. Okay. Thank you very much. So we have a pretty lengthy presentation for you today, so I apologize for that in advance. The outline of this is going to be, we're going to give you some background to this phase two data center work that we're doing.

[01:42:49] We're going to talk about the county's authority in zoning regulations, the proposed zoning overlay, excuse me, the existing and proposed performance regulations, the proposed zoning overlay district, some taxation information from the finance department, Some discussion about the public input, and then we have some very specific focused questions that we'd like to get some guidance from you on, and then discuss the next steps.

[01:43:18] So to get started, the text amendment would establish standards for all data centers, and the zoning map amendment would create the overlay districts. And I'm going to talk in more detail about both the ordinance and the overlay districts in a minute.

[01:43:35] So data centers are places that house computer systems that process large amounts of information. They can provide for a more diversified economy and tax base.

[01:43:52] They do have some unique features, including the need for uninterrupted power, and they consume large amounts of power. The equipment in data centers generates a significant amount of heat that requires cooling. They can be in large buildings, and they typically require fewer staff per square foot than other industrial or office spaces. They can support local economies and enable innovation and operate with minimal disruption to surrounding communities. Data centers often have lower sewer demands than other industrial uses. Depending on the cooling method, they can have lower or higher water demand

[01:44:32] than other uses, and I'm going to talk about that in detail later. They typically generate fewer truck trips compared to most other industrial uses once they're operational. So what can the county do in regards to data centers? It's a typical zoning thing. We can limit the zoning districts where data centers can be located. We can establish setbacks, size requirements, height requirements, noise limits, landscaping, the standard zoning toolkit. What we cannot do

[01:45:06] is we cannot regulate based on energy consumption or the type of energy that they use, the employment, the customers they serve, the technology they use, or the origin of the equipment used to operate the center.

[01:45:23] So let's talk about the proposed ordinance. I'm going to move on to the discussion of the proposed ordinance in the overlay districts. All of the discussion that follows is based on extensive research the team has done. We have reviewed numerous ordinances and talked to other localities and water authorities to determine what has and what has not worked.

[01:45:45] So the starting point is the county currently allows data centers up to 40,000 square feet on property with industrial zoning. Over 40,000 square feet in industrial zoning, it requires a special use permit. And of any size in commercial districts, it requires a special use permit.

[01:46:06] The proposed regulations, I'm going to go through different series of regulations. I broke them out to try to put them in bite-sized pieces for you. So setbacks, the proposed regulations maintain the existing regulations outside of the proposed data center overlay district.

[01:46:24] The only proposed change would be that there is no increased setback within the district. So 200 feet from any lot line would only apply outside of the district. Inside the district, the standard district regulations would apply. But still the 200 feet from properties outside the district and 500 feet from rural area zoning.

[01:46:46] Moving on to generators, the current regulations, they have to be enclosed in sound dampening enclosure, and they have limited hours of operation except during power outage. Under the proposed regulations, we would maintain those existing regulations.

[01:47:03] We would also only allow natural gas or low-emission generators, and battery energy storage systems would be permitted. For the landscaping and screening, under the existing regulations, landscaping requirement can include two rows of trees.

[01:47:23] That's the existing landscaping requirement. If it's in an entrance corridor, there could be some more, but just sticking with the standard landscaping requirements. The proposed regulation would be increasing planting requirements. It'd be three rows of evergreen trees, and that would be from an approved list.

[01:47:42] The county could determine where those trees would be planted, so that it would be the most appropriate location. And if fencing is provided, it must be between the building and the screening, so it can't be outside. The fence needs to be screened also.

[01:48:01] Building design. Currently, the only regulations are on the entrance corridor districts, and what is proposed is a requirement that would break up blank walls so that there would be recesses, windows, changes in texture, color, and materials, things like that.

[01:48:19] And that equipment would be contained within an enclosed building or courtyard. So, water regulations. If water is used, the current regulation is if water is used for cooling, it must be in a closed loop or recycled system.

[01:48:37] The proposed regulations that you have before you in the packet would be that coordination would be required between the Rivanna Service Authority and Albemarle County Service Authority when you're doing your initial filling of the system or any flushing or maintenance of that system.

[01:48:58] We've been doing a lot more research since the preparation of the packet. After further study and discussion with several water authorities with large numbers of data staff is going to be recommending some additional language we don't have the exact language yet but what we're going to be getting at is that there be no groundwater use permitted it's implied now but we want to make that explicit that no use of evaporative systems occur and that's what really drives the the water uses is the evaporative system we're still working on the exact language we want to define what a closed loop system is what recycled water is we've

[01:49:37] We've looked at a variety of different ordinance regulations from various localities. We can do it, and what we've discovered is that with the right types of regulations and the right kind of design, data centers consume water at or lower than a comparably sized office building.

[01:49:57] So we're still working on that. I just want to let you know we don't have the exact language. We know we can do it. We just don't know what the exact best language is right now. Noise regulations. The county currently does have regulations for all uses, all industrial uses included.

[01:50:15] The proposed regulations include more restrictive noise standards than contained in the existing ordinance. The maximum daytime limit in the ordinance right now is 60 dBA, and the maximum nighttime limit is 55 dBA. This is the most restrictive standard that's currently in the ordinance.

[01:50:35] What we're proposing is that this standard would apply to data centers regardless of the designation of the adjoining property. The standard changes whether it's a commercial property or an industrial property that's adjacent. So you can be louder next to a commercial or an industrial building.

[01:50:52] We're proposing that no matter what, they have to use the lowest standard period. It would also require a sound study to be submitted prior to approval, And then it would require post-construction monitoring, testing, to see whether or not they're achieving the sounds that they were permitted to have.

[01:51:15] And that if not, they must take corrective measures. There are also proposed regulations for increased setbacks in landscaping. So all those things combined are really the noise regulations. So I'm going to move on to the overlay district now.

[01:51:35] So I've discussed the proposed regulations. They would apply to data centers in any overlay district or outside of any overlay district. So now we're going to talk about just the data centers. In our consideration of how to appropriately allow data centers, we focused on criteria for that overlay district that assures adequate infrastructure, appropriate land use designations, and locations that would limit impacts.

[01:52:00] All of the areas in the proposed overlay district are already designated for some type of intense development. And data centers would have more requirements than any other uses currently permitted on those properties. And the criteria we have had to be in the development area, had to be recommended for industrial research and development or some similar type of use in the comprehensive plan.

[01:52:24] Needed to be served by public water and sewer and needed to be close to high voltage power lines. In many cases, the high voltage power lines is actually on the property or inside the district. So what could happen, and we're not recommending any particular tier, but we wanted to give you an example of the kinds of opportunities that you have that you could tier these should you desire.

[01:52:53] You could have one area as a tier one, one area as a tier two, and we have 40,000 square feet, which is the existing regulation. That would apply outside of any district, and that is a footprint, and one of the things we're proposing is a definition of footprint because, believe it or not, we don't have one.

[01:58:33] Thank you, Bill. Board of Afternoon, Jacob Sumner, Chief Financial Officer for the county. And my portion of this presentation is to talk a little bit about the financial impact of data centers and particularly on the revenue generation component for the locality.

[01:58:49] I first want to start just with a reminder of our budget conversations back in the spring. And we talked a lot about the diversifying our tax base and our current reliance on our residential taxpayer, particularly the real estate. And the goal in moving forward, looking at how do we diversify our tax base and really start finding ways to balance out that residential and commercial tax base for our community.

[01:59:16] Data centers can be a tool in helping find that diversification. It's an option, and it's not the only option, but it is an option and a tool that can help in addressing that balance. Data centers do provide two revenue streams for localities.

[01:59:35] First is a tax on the real estate, as you imagine, and can tell that this would be on the land that a data center would be on, but also include the improvements, which is really the buildings that house the data center itself. The second revenue stream would be the business tangible personal property.

[01:59:55] This represents the servers, the racks, the other peripheral computer equipment that essentially makes the data center run, and that's the equipment that's housed inside the building itself. This equipment is a substantial investment in data center development, and it's particularly to note that this business tangible personal property is a depreciating asset.

[02:00:20] And so unlike the real estate, which typically holds value over time, the business tangible personal property and how we assess it quickly depreciates from about 25% of the original cost of that equipment and depreciates over seven years until you get to about 10%.

[02:00:35] So there is, over time, some decrease in, I won't say volatility, but a decrease in the tax revenues over the depreciable life of those assets. I will say the useful life of most data center equipment is between five and seven years, all really depending on the intensity of the use and how often that equipment, how often those servers need to be replenished.

[02:01:02] and that can be taken into account when we go to look at these properties and look at these tax sources. The other thing I wanted to mention on this slide before we move on to my next slide is back in May the board received a presentation on the cost of community services ratios and particularly that was focused on how much does it cost to provide services per dollar of revenue generated from different categories. So for instance, the county's ratios that were presented for residential revenues, for every dollar of residential revenue that was generated,

[02:01:39] there's a cost of about $1.31 to provide services to our residential community. The commercial ratio is 0.32. So about every dollar of revenue generated from commercial tax base costs about 32 cents to service that community as well. Data centers and the research that we've done have an even lower cost of community services ratio than our current commercial.

[02:02:05] Now, we've seen these as low as 0.1, so every dollar of tax revenue generated would or could potentially have a cost of service of 10 cents or even less in some localities. And this is really driven by, as Bill mentioned earlier, there's, you know, data centers typically have lowered vehicular traffic.

[02:02:25] There are fewer employees compared to other commercial uses, and all this just requires fewer county services provided to the property. So if we go to the next slide, we did want to provide some scenarios so that the board and the community would have some ideas of what the tax revenue generation potentials would have with the different size districts that Bill mentioned earlier in his presentation.

[04:33:19] In this 2018 collection, VDOT estimated 130 vehicles used Dudley Mountain Road per day. If this special use permit were to be approved by the board, there would be an estimated 590 vehicles on Dudley Mountain Road per day. This figure was generated by adding the applicant's estimated data and the 2018 VDOT ADT data.

[04:33:39] According to the VDOT road design standards for rural local roads, there are minimum requirements based on the traffic volume on a given road. For an ADT under 400 vehicles per day, the minimum width of pavement is 18 feet and the minimum width of a graded shoulder is 2 feet.

[04:33:57] If the ADT is between 400 and 2,000 vehicles per day, the minimum width is increased to 20 feet and the minimum width of a graded shoulder is 3 feet. There are other factors such as minimum stopping site distance and minimum radius along the road that are also considered in the standards for rural local roads.

[04:34:14] As is, Dudley Mountain Road's width pavement is highly variable. According to VDOT measurements, the road has sections less than 15 feet and as wide as 20 feet. Additionally, the road has a substandard shoulder or no shoulder at all. As is with existing traffic volume of 130 vehicles per day, Dudley Mountain Road does not meet the VDOT road design standards for rural local roads with an ADT under 400 vehicles per day. If the board approves the special use permit for the private school, it would generate 590 vehicles per day and add additional traffic on a road that does not already meet VDOT standards. [05:18:53] We pivoted to a forest school model embracing outdoor learning to keep our doors open for families. This was not just about surviving a crisis.
